A clean scan means the tool found nothing. It is not proof of security. The secret scan above is a pattern search written for this review, weaker than a dedicated scanner. The same holds for the tests: 414 passing security tests show that 414 specific attacks fail, not that the utilities are secure, and the mutation runs show only that those tests notice twenty particular ways of breaking eight controls (seven in the sixth round, thirteen in the seventh). The comparison with the database driver covers the two installed versions and the strings that were generated, nothing more. No secret scan was repeated after the first review, and no dependency audit between the first re-check and 2026-10-12; commits 28c92d1 and e2a4a7a change no dependency (`git show e2a4a7a --stat` lists no manifest or lockfile), and the fixes of SEC-020 and SEC-021 touch `src/server/http.ts`, its test, and one security test each (`git status --short`). The catalog cases of 2026-10-13 replace the database client with a recorder: they prove the shape of every query the six routes send and the allowlist of every answer, not which rows PostgreSQL returns for those conditions.

## Residual risks

Authentication and sessions (T-015) and the admin bootstrap (T-016) were reviewed on 2026-10-13 ([reviews/T-015-T-016-security.md](reviews/T-015-T-016-security.md)); what that review did not cover is listed at its end. The fixes of SEC-033 to SEC-035 were re-checked the same day ([reviews/SEC-033-035-recheck.md](reviews/SEC-033-035-recheck.md)), with its own list of what was not covered, and so were the fixes of SEC-037 and SEC-038 ([reviews/SEC-037-038-recheck.md](reviews/SEC-037-038-recheck.md)) and of SEC-039, SEC-040, and the start-up validation of `AUTH_SECRET` (CR-66) ([reviews/SEC-039-040-recheck.md](reviews/SEC-039-040-recheck.md)), and of SEC-043 to SEC-045 ([reviews/SEC-043-045-recheck.md](reviews/SEC-043-045-recheck.md)), and of SEC-046 to SEC-048 ([reviews/SEC-046-047-recheck.md](reviews/SEC-046-047-recheck.md)), and of SEC-049 on 2026-10-14 ([reviews/SEC-049-recheck.md](reviews/SEC-049-recheck.md)), and of SEC-050 the same day ([reviews/SEC-050-recheck.md](reviews/SEC-050-recheck.md)), and of SEC-041, SEC-042, CR-57 (sign-up signs in, decoy cookie for a duplicate), and the `/sign-in` redirect the same day ([reviews/SEC-042-041-CR57-recheck.md](reviews/SEC-042-041-CR57-recheck.md); CR-57 at unit level with a mocked database and a stub library, its integration cases never run). The catalog module (T-020, commit 9f7301a and the follow-ups to cd50e34), the app shell and the `UserName` component (T-018, commits 1018fde, 54a070a, 24e1577), and the CR-83 request frame and auth runtime change (6cb6466, 0769f7c) were reviewed on 2026-10-13 with unit-level tests only, because Docker was down ([reviews/T-020-T-018-security.md](reviews/T-020-T-018-security.md)); no new finding. What that review could not run is listed at its end; above all, no catalog query was run against PostgreSQL by the security agent, so that the visibility conditions select the right rows rests on `tests/integration/catalog` (agent-reported, not re-run). Not security-reviewed at all yet: the database schema and client, and the integration-test harness.

Not reviewed, because it does not exist yet: authorization in services and tenant isolation (the seller application and own-store endpoints exist since T-040 and were reviewed on 2026-10-15, [reviews/T-040-security.md](reviews/T-040-security.md); the seller upload endpoint (T-041) on 2026-10-19; the seller product and inventory endpoints (T-042) on 2026-10-20, [reviews/T-042-security.md](reviews/T-042-security.md); the seller order, fulfilment, and summary endpoints (T-043) on 2026-10-20, [reviews/T-043-security.md](reviews/T-043-security.md); the admin endpoints (T-050) on 2026-10-23, [reviews/T-050-security.md](reviews/T-050-security.md), and the admin pages (T-051) on 2026-10-23, [reviews/T-051-security.md](reviews/T-051-security.md); the catalog is public and takes no actor; the cart takes an actor and its per-user ownership was reviewed on 2026-10-14; checkout and the customer's orders were reviewed on 2026-10-14, see the T-032 entry below), uploads and file serving (catalog image URLs point at `/api/files/{key}`, which answers 404 until T-041), CORS handling, security headers (TM-23; built in T-072 and reviewed on 2026-10-26, see the paragraph on headers below), and CI workflows.

Public catalog reads (T-020): none is rate-limited, by contract (API_CONTRACTS 5.2.1). Each list request runs a count and a page; a search runs `ILIKE '%q%'` on titles without a trigram index, so one client can make the server scan the product table as often as it likes (code-review CR-88, D-013 accepted for a small catalog). Bounded per request (q at most 100 characters, page size at most 50, a page past the end sends no `OFFSET` query), not in number. Upstream rate limiting or a search index is needed before the catalog grows. The product detail gives the exact `stock` to anyone (API_CONTRACTS 5.2, BR-30, by design). Reviews stay public, without a name, when their author is later deactivated; no requirement says otherwise.

Framework advisories (SEC-089, re-checked 2026-10-26, [reviews/SEC-089-recheck.md](reviews/SEC-089-recheck.md)): `next` 16.4.3 is outside the audited vulnerable range, but the texts of GHSA-2464-fhr4-w9xw, GHSA-673q-mjm6-r5xr, and GHSA-p3m5-59mp-jv5r were never read, so any configuration step they require is unknown. The `/_next/image` optimiser is still served with framework defaults; `cacheComponents` is on. Dependency safety beyond the advisory database (malicious releases, unpublished defects) is not checked by `pnpm audit`. Development-only advisories in the Prisma CLI and `jsdom` stay open under SEC-010.

Exists and has had no security review: the database schema and its migration (T-013), the database client `src/server/db`, the integration-test harness (`tests/helpers`, `tests/integration`; its refusal rules were code-reviewed, CR-25 to CR-35, not security-reviewed), `next.config.ts` other than its headers (reviewed 2026-10-26, T-072), and the production build output (client bundle contents).

Security headers (T-072, reviewed 2026-10-26, [reviews/T-072-security.md](reviews/T-072-security.md)): accepted by the owner in D-046, the CSP allows inline script and style, so it does not stop an injected `<script>` or event-handler attribute; React escaping and the absence of `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` on user content are the only XSS controls, and every new page or component must keep that. Header values are computed at `next build`: `APP_URL` must be the https origin at build time or `upgrade-insecure-requests` is silently left out; HSTS `includeSubDomains` forces HTTPS on every subdomain of the deployed host (deployment dependent). No CSP violation reporting exists. Not checked: the project's own responses under `next start` or over HTTPS, `/_next/static` and `/_next/image` responses, and a browser run of the CSP. SEC-091 is FIXED (re-checked 2026-10-26). The uploaded-file sandbox policy now depends on the order of the rules in `next.config.ts`, with the `/api/files/:path*` rule after `/:path*`. `tests/security/sec091-recheck.test.ts` guards that order. It was not observed under `next start` on the project itself. A reverse proxy or CDN that rewrites `Content-Security-Policy` would also undo it (deployment dependent).

Reviews (T-035, reviewed 2026-10-26, [reviews/T-035-security.md](reviews/T-035-security.md)): ownership, one review per item, delivered only, and injection hold on real PostgreSQL. Residual: review text cannot be hidden or removed (SEC-092, open); fake reviews through a second account are cheap (SEC-072, open; T-035 added no guard); one user may review one product once per order item, so repeat purchases give repeat ratings (BR-11 by design); the `review-create` limit is per process and per user (10 an hour), so many accounts or several server instances multiply it; the service relies on the fulfilment table for "delivered implies paid" and does not re-check payment itself. Not reviewed: the frontend review form (in progress during the review) and the order page's display of a review. Of T-011, the SEC-009 refusals, the start-up hook, and the health check were re-checked on 2026-10-12; the other rules of `src/server/env.ts` were read, not attacked.

Sign-up limit override and header session (session 116, reviewed 2026-10-27, [reviews/S116-security.md](reviews/S116-security.md)): `SIGN_UP_RATE_LIMIT` (1 to 1000) is refused at start when the runtime `NODE_ENV` is `production`, and any `NODE_ENV` other than `development`, `test`, `production` is refused. A deployment whose runtime `NODE_ENV` is unset, `development`, or `test` accepts it (and with it loses every other production check of env.ts); the Docker image sets `NODE_ENV=production`, so the risk is an operator override. Since 039613d (SEC-093, re-checked 2026-10-27) a `next build` output refuses the override a second time from the inlined build-time `NODE_ENV`, whatever the runtime value; CR-S116-14 (preview or staging with another `NODE_ENV`) is closed by that and by the `NODE_ENV` enum. Remaining: a server started with `next dev` accepts the override (that is how e2e runs, on purpose); `next dev` is not a deployment mode and lacks every other production check. An explicit e2e marker variable was judged not to add an independent layer (same channel, same operator). In a production build with the override set and a wrong runtime `NODE_ENV`, every auth route (sign-up, sign-in, sign-out, session) answers 500 until the variable is removed: fail closed, but a sign-in outage caused by configuration (re-checked 2026-10-27, session 120). The override limiter counts in the same process table as `rateLimiter` (tested). The header reads the session with `getActor` on every storefront, seller, admin, and not-found render and passes only the display name and a cart line count to the client; a broken session read shows the signed-out links (display only). Not checked: the header under `next start`, the RSC payload of a real page, and a browser.

Not verified in this review:

- How module state behaves across module instances in a running server (review item V6). Measured by devops with Next.js 16.4.0 in both modes: the start-up hook and the route handlers are compiled separately and each has its own copy of a module. What follows from that was judged on 2026-10-12 by reading and with a second copy of each module inside one test process, not in a running server: the logger's level is shared (fixed in commit b40abf0); the rate limiter's counters, sweep time, and saturation-log times are shared since commit f66ab8c (SEC-030, fixed and re-checked with module copies in one test process; not in a running server, and not across realms); `instanceof AppError` is false across copies, so an `AppError` made by one copy is a generic 500 for another (fails closed, tested); the health check's probe and log limit are per copy, with one importer today. Whether two route handlers, or a route handler and the proxy file, share one copy was not measured by anyone; it needs two routes and a running server.
- Whether the framework adds or changes `Cache-Control` on route-handler responses (the helpers themselves were verified, SEC-006).
- Every caller-side rule beyond the four auth routes (checked 2026-10-13 for those four only): that handlers pass the SR-01 configuration to `getClientAddress` and derive `trustIncoming` from it, that a per-email limit is consumed only after the per-address check, that only `clientIp` carries an address in a log line, and that nobody logs a payload (SR-04).
- `src/server/env.ts` validates `TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS` and `TRUSTED_CLIENT_IP_HEADER` at start-up since T-011. Its rules were read against SR-01 on 2026-10-12 and meet the three requirements written there; the security agent wrote no adversarial tests for them. Nothing reads the validated values yet: no caller passes `getEnv().trustedProxy` to `getClientAddress` or `appOrigin` to `assertSameOrigin`.
- The validated `LOG_LEVEL` reaches every logger of the process since commit b40abf0: `loadEnvAtStartup` calls `setLogLevel`, and the level is kept on `globalThis` under a key of the symbol registry, so every copy of the logger module reads it. Re-checked on 2026-10-12 with two copies of the module in one test process; that the level set in the start-up hook reaches a route handler of a running server is by the implementer's run in development mode, not repeated by the security agent and not run in production mode by anyone. Nothing a request can send sets the slot (a parsed JSON document has no symbol key), and a slot that is absent, holds anything but one of the four levels, or throws when read gives the default `info` (tested). Code inside the process can set it, also through a symbol-keyed property of `Object.prototype`, which the read does not exclude; that needs code execution and is noted only. A level of `error` hides every `warn` and `info` line: the line for a body that broke off and the recovery line of the health check (both allowed). The saturation warning of the rate limiter is written through `logger.security()` and is not hidden (SEC-031, fixed). The SR-01 start-up warning still uses `warn` and is written before the level is applied; it is safe by that order only until it moves to `security()`.
- The exit of the start-up hook on an invalid environment (`src/instrumentation.ts`) is requested from the callback of one write to standard output, and since commit b123ef9 a timer of 2 seconds that is not unref'd ends the process when that callback does not come (code-review finding CR-44). Read on 2026-10-12, and the exit on a refused environment was observed on the production build; a blocked or closed pipe was not reproduced by anyone. Until the exit happens (at most 2 seconds) the framework has printed "Ready"; by devops' measurement it answers 500 in that state.
- A failure of the import inside the hook (`await import("./server/env")` stands outside the `try`) is thrown to the framework without ending the process, and under `next start` the framework then stays up and answers 500. That is a defect of the build, not of the configuration, and it fails closed; noted for devops.
- What the Prisma CLI loads of the packages named in SEC-010.
- T-034 UI (checkout, order confirmation, order history; commit cb07d84), reviewed 2026-10-22 by reading and with static and wire-level tests ([reviews/T-034-security.md](reviews/T-034-security.md)). Not done: a browser run, an e2e run, a look at the built client bundle, and a run of the pages against a database; that another user's order is a 404 rests on `findOwnedOrder` (T-032 review and its integration tests), not on a new run.
- Import guards, state on 2026-10-23 (fourth re-check, commit 84b93f6): the spellings of the first three re-checks are rejected; still passing, and registered: a literal name behind a TypeScript wrapper or a default value (SEC-085), percent-encoded, NTFS-stream, and `data:` specifiers (SEC-086), and a two-hop exit from `src` through the root or a new top-level folder (SEC-087). The owner-accepted residual (D-045) stands. Run-time effect inside a Next.js bundle was not tested for any spelling. The history below is kept for reference.
- Import guards (CR-T032-10, 2026-10-22; SEC-081 re-checked twice the same day). Since 0a7ea5e the guard rejects the 23 spellings of SEC-081 and the 19 of `tests/security/sec081-recheck.test.ts`. Still passing (SEC-081 OPEN): an inline disable in any file outside `src`, and 7 loader spellings through aliases of `module`, `globalThis`, `global`, `Reflect`, a stored `.constructor`, and `__non_webpack_require__` (`tests/security/sec081-recheck-2.test.ts`). Application code may import `scripts/`, `tests/`, `prisma/seed.ts`, and `e2e/`, which reach the db layer (SEC-083 OPEN). Vitest-run `src/**/*.test.ts(x)` files are exempt from the loader selectors by design; that is safe only while no application file can import a test-named module (`testImportPattern`, name-based). Whether any lint-passing spelling actually yields the database client inside a Next.js bundle at run time was not tested. The probes show that the listed spellings are caught, not that every spelling is: `eval`, `new Function`, `process.mainModule`, a `typeof import()` type query (no run-time effect), and a module that hands out a database value from inside a function (for example returning `db`) are not checked. The payment-transition check is a test over syntax, not a lint rule or a type: a file that passes the orders module object along through an allowed importer is caught only where it imports it. The transitions stay on the public surface of `src/modules/orders/index.ts`; moving them to a file the public surface does not re-export, with a lint restriction on that path, would make the limit structural (backend-api, orchestrator; suggested, not registered as a finding).
- The other changes of commits 4407b71 and 28c92d1 that are not security findings (the `ZodError` boundary, field-path escaping, `invalidHeader`, documentation wording) were read but are the code reviewer's to judge.
- Whether the framework itself limits the size of a route-handler request body, in what chunk sizes it hands a body to a handler, how long it lets a body stall, and what it does with the connection after a 413 (SEC-019, SEC-020, SR-05). The helper was tested directly and behind a plain `node:http` server on the loopback interface, not through the framework.
- The caller-side rules of SR-05, because no handler calls `readJsonBody`: that no handler reads a body any other way, that the order is the one of SR-05 item 5, and that a raised limit is documented per endpoint. The order exists as a documented pattern only: the file comment of `src/server/http.ts` shows the ten steps since 2026-10-12, and it agrees with section 1.8 of the contract and with SR-05 item 5 (compared on 2026-10-12; a security test compares the comment with the contract's table), but a comment enforces nothing, and the names of the two helpers of step 4 are placeholders until T-015. A lint rule or a test that enumerates the handlers and proves the order on each is needed when they exist; so far a security test checks only that no `route.ts` reads a body by itself.
- SR-06 and SR-07 as a whole: nothing collects logs and no session code exists, so retention, log access, and session removal are requirements without an implementation or a check.
- (Settled 2026-10-13.) The auth library's own routes are not mounted; the four application routes call `assertSameOrigin`, `assertContentType`, the limiter, and `readJsonBody` in the SR-05 order. The library's address detection and rate limiter are off. The per-email subject is read from the parsed body after the per-address limit (S20). Reviewed in [reviews/T-015-T-016-security.md](reviews/T-015-T-016-security.md).

Known and standing:

- In-process rate limiting does not protect a multi-instance or serverless deployment.
- The process-wide limiter trusts its `globalThis` slot (SEC-030, accepted 2026-10-12): code running in the server process can plant a table, edit counters, or put something malformed there, which is replaced by an empty table and resets every limit once. A client cannot reach the slot. Only the exported `rateLimiter` is shared; a limiter made with `createRateLimiter()` counts for its own module copy, so production code must not create its own (nothing enforces this; a lint rule or a test that greps for `createRateLimiter` outside tests would).
- `logger.security()` is not filtered by `LOG_LEVEL` (SR-04). Once T-015 writes a line per failed sign-in or `Origin` failure, the volume of those lines is bounded only by the rate limit that counts the event and by the proxy, not by the operator's level. Each such event must be behind a rate limit, and log storage sized for a flood (SR-06).
- A `security()` line looks like any other line of its level; nothing marks it for an alert rule (SR-04). Operators who alert on the saturation warning match its message, `Rate limit store saturated`.
- The limiter uses fixed windows: a client can make up to twice the limit across a window boundary (for example 10 sign-in attempts in two seconds, then none for a minute).
- The limiter fails closed (SR-02): a flood from very many distinct networks can make one rule refuse new subjects while it lasts. Upstream protection (proxy or CDN rate limiting) is needed for that and is not planned.
- A per-email sign-in limit lets anyone with enough addresses lock a known email out of signing in. Since the SEC-032 fix (2026-10-13, re-checked 2026-10-14) one address uses only its own allowance for an account (`sign-in-email` on address and email), and the account-wide `sign-in-account` (60 per 300 s) cannot be exhausted by one or two addresses before the owner's first attempt in a window: it takes at least three addresses at their strongest timing, or 12 at five a minute, or one IPv6 /48 (SR-02). The owner's own attempts count in the same window, so against two addresses an owner who mistypes can be refused until the window ends. Filling the whole `sign-in-account` rule refuses sign-in for every email without a live window: since the SEC-050 fix (D-038, re-checked 2026-10-14) that takes 50,000 emails inside 5 minutes, about 167 a second from 2,000 networks at the per-address limit, which one IPv6 /48 or a modest pool of IPv4 addresses provides. That is five times the cost before the fix but a quarter of the cost before SEC-032 (8,000 networks), and below the "tens of thousands" of SR-02's accepted remainder; the owner has not accepted that number in writing beyond D-038's test. Admin accounts are reachable the same way. An attacker behind the owner's own address (carrier-grade NAT) shares the owner's per-address allowances. Counting only failures would not remove the lock-out, because the attacker's attempts are failures; a per-device allowance (OWASP "device cookie") would, and is not planned for the MVP.
- The sign-in and sign-up response floor (SEC-035, 250 ms plus 0 to 25 ms) hides the timing difference only while the library's work (scrypt, inserts) stays below it. Under load, when that work takes longer, nothing is added and the difference shows again; the per-address and per-email limits bound the number of samples. A successful sign-in is not padded. Measured on loopback only (re-check 2026-10-13), not through a proxy or under concurrent load. Since CR-57 the new-email sign-up also inserts a session and reads twice more than the duplicate path, so the work the floor must cover is larger; and the first duplicate after a process start also hashes the equaliser's fixed text once (three scrypt operations against two, under the floor while one takes less than about 80 ms). Not measured: the integration timing case has not run since CR-57.
- Account enumeration through sign-up (TM-03, D-037): the sign-up answer is alike for a new and a registered email, but the cookie it carries is a session only for a new one, so `GET /api/v1/auth/session` tells them apart. Before CR-57 a sign-in with the password just chosen did the same. Cost: one `sign-up` allowance (5 an hour per address) per probe, and every unregistered address probed becomes an account. Closing it needs email verification. A browser holding the decoy passes the proxy's cookie-presence check and is refused by the page guards.
- Names: the schema refuses format characters, line separators, and blank-looking names since the SEC-037 fix (re-checked 2026-10-13), and since the SEC-039 fix (re-checked 2026-10-13) also unassigned, private-use, and default-ignorable characters and variation selectors other than U+FE0E and U+FE0F; since the run limit (ae8e0d3, re-checked 2026-10-14) it refuses a run of four or more combining marks or U+200B to U+200D, but still accepts U+FE0E and U+FE0F after any letter and runs of up to three kept characters between visible ones (SEC-041; U+200B to U+200D by owner decision for Thai), so two names can differ invisibly and a name can carry about 7 hidden bits per visible character; a precomposed letter plus three marks still draws 5 or 6 marks on one base (SEC-042 display part, clipping not built); the limit refuses syllables of other scripts with four marks in a row, for example the Burmese U+1001 U+103B U+103D U+1031 U+1038 (owner's choice for Thai; usability, not security); names are not normalised (a name in NFD and the same name in NFC are different strings); look-alike visible characters (confusables, for example Cyrillic letters in a Latin name) are still accepted. Every screen that shows a name must render it isolated (`<bdi>`, SEC-033 rendering part), and every later name field (store, address) must reuse the schema rule.
- Name rendering (SEC-033 rendering part, re-checked 2026-10-14 at 4fa4352): the storefront isolates store names, the display name, address fields, and the search query. Nothing enforces this for new code except `tests/security/sec033-rendering-recheck.test.ts`. That file checks only that each `<UserName>` holds one plain value, and it renders the product card and the product detail. It does not find a name that a new component renders with no `UserName` at all. There is no lint rule. Product titles are isolated in `<bdi>` since 004c213 (SEC-057, re-checked 2026-10-14), and a static case fails when any read of a `title` or `productTitle` field (any variable name, or a destructured binding) under `src/app` or `src/components` is outside `UserName` or a stripped FSI ... PDI wrap, unless it is pinned with a reason in `TITLE_READ_EXCLUSIONS` (syntax-tree scan since 2026-10-21, CR-107; the earlier scan matched only the names `product`, `item`, `line`). The scan does not follow data flow: a module DTO that renames the field (for example `heading: row.title` in `src/modules`) and is rendered as `{x.heading}` is not seen, and the generic `title` props of layout and `ui/` parts are pinned on the basis that every caller passes Thai copy today. The image `alt` wrap strips isolate controls from the title since b411e34, and a static case fails when an FSI ... PDI wrap in `src/app` or `src/components` does not call `stripIsolateControls`; a title that holds a paragraph separator still ends the wrap (SEC-057, still open). Store names and product titles have no write path yet, so no schema refuses controls in them; the database accepts anything. Category names (admin-written) and descriptions and review comments (free text, each in its own paragraph) are not isolated, by design. Text in attributes (`aria-label`, `alt`) and in document titles cannot use `<bdi>`: the recipient name in the address buttons' `aria-label` is safe only because its schema refuses controls. The search query accepts bidi controls; they are contained by the `<bdi>`. Not checked in a browser or with a screen reader. Pages that do not exist yet (cart, checkout, orders, seller and admin dashboards) must follow the same rule.
- The `__Host-` protection (SEC-034) depends on browsers applying the prefix rules; it was not tested in a browser. Padded cookie names that the library's parser would trim are dropped before the library reads cookies (SEC-038 fixed, re-checked 2026-10-13); this holds only while every hand-over of request headers to the library goes through `headersForAuthLibrary` (a static case in `tests/security/sec037-038-recheck.test.ts` checks the `auth.api` calls under `src`; it does not see a call written another way, such as a destructured `api`), and a later library version that parses cookies through another path would need the same filter. Whether any browser stores and sends a cookie name with a raw 0xA0 byte is still not tested; with the filter it no longer matters for the session cookie.
- Sessions last 7 days from sign-in with no idle timeout and no re-authentication before admin actions; a stolen admin cookie is usable for up to 7 days unless the admin signs out or is deactivated.
- The admin bootstrap writes no audit row (DATA_MODEL section 4.1); the only trace of an admin's creation is the script's output and the row's `createdAt`. The script loads `.env` when present, so a bootstrap password put there stays on disk.
- `AUTH_SECRET` is validated at start-up since CR-66 (re-checked 2026-10-13): missing, shorter than 32 UTF-16 units, or, in production, containing `not_a_secret` stops the start, by name and rule. Since SEC-044 (re-checked 2026-10-13) production also refuses fewer than 8 distinct code points, an exact repetition, and the two published values; since SEC-047 (re-checked 2026-10-13) it also refuses characters outside printable ASCII, a repeated 8-character window, an 8-character run in constant steps, and the published values in any case or inside a longer value. Since SEC-049 (re-checked 2026-10-14) it also refuses a value where one code-point difference at a lag of 1 to 8 covers at least half of the positions, and values that start with `better-auth-secret` or `integration-test-secret` in any case. Still accepted: the published values re-spelled with other separators and lightly edited (SEC-051, open), a keyboard row, word phrases, a pattern with a period of 9 or more that changes at least two characters per period, and a patterned half followed by a random half (that half carries the entropy); no rule can prove a chosen value random. A phrase of short words joined by dashes is refused about 3 or 4 times in 10,000 (lag rule), so such a generator needs a retry. Since SEC-045 and SEC-046 (re-checked 2026-10-13) the library takes its signing secret only from `createAuth`, and seven library variables, the telemetry ones included, are refused; the list is pinned to better-auth 1.7.7 by a test and must be re-listed on an update. Since SEC-048 (re-checked 2026-10-13) `errorResponse` logs any unexpected error by class name, status, and code only. Outside `errorResponse` that does not hold: the health check logs up to 300 characters of the driver's message (SEC-026, accepted), the auth library's logger writes the library's log message (which can quote a request URL), anything thrown outside a handler's `try` is printed by the framework (since CR-83, commits 6cb6466 and 0769f7c, re-checked 2026-10-13, the auth runtime and the catalog's configuration reads are inside it; what remains outside is `connection()`, the request id, and the child logger; the request id's read of the configuration, `requestIdTrust()`, has its own `catch` and falls back to a generated id), and a thrown `Proxy` whose `getPrototypeOf` trap throws makes `errorResponse` itself throw. The length rule counts code points (zod 4.6.5), not UTF-16 units as the comment in `env.ts` says. That the start-up hook really ends `next start` on a refused secret was not run in this re-check (the hook's exit was observed by devops for other variables). No rotation of the secret is supported: changing it ends every session.
- With the default proxy configuration all clients share one per-address bucket (SR-01). Correct limits in any deployment depend on `TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS` or `TRUSTED_CLIENT_IP_HEADER` being set correctly and on the application port not being reachable around the proxy. A wrong hop count either trusts a client-written address or rate-limits the proxy itself.
- Carrier-grade NAT, offices, and campuses put many users behind one IPv4 address; they share a per-address allowance.
- One holder of an IPv6 /48 controls 65,536 /64 networks, more than the 40,000 windows of an anonymous rule, and can keep `sign-up` (about 11 requests a second), `sign-in-address` (about 670 a second), or `sign-in-account` (about 167 a second from 2,000 of its networks) refusing new subjects. Nothing fails open and existing subjects are unaffected (SR-02, capacity split).
- The signed-in rules (`checkout`, `seller-application`, `review-create`; `uploads` needs approved sellers) are filled by 10,000 accounts each, and one client can keep them full once the accounts exist (SEC-052, open, Medium). `checkout` calls the limiter since T-032, so for checkout this is exploitable as soon as the route is deployed. `seller-application` has a caller since T-040 (`POST /seller/application`, keyed by the session's user id; filling it keeps new applicants out, not existing sellers). The other two rules have no caller yet.
- Addresses that a proxy writes in an unusual form share a subject: hexadecimal IPv4-mapped addresses are `unknown`, written-out mapped addresses and loopback share the all-zero /64, and all NAT64 clients share one /64 (SR-01).
- Logger redaction is by key name and text pattern. A secret or personal value under an unlisted key, or inside free text in a shape the patterns do not know, is still written. The control is that callers log ids, not payloads (SR-04).
- Shapes the logger is known not to mask, observed on 2026-10-11 and accepted in SR-04: a bare JWT or other token with no key in front of it; a header name and its value as two separate array items; `user:password@host` without a scheme; a value on the line after its key; a lower-case standalone `basic` credential; `file://user:password@host`; emails without a dotted domain, with a quoted local part, an address literal, or a percent-encoded `@`; a key written in full-width letters; a boxed `String` object; personal data under a key that is on no list (for example `dob`, `nationalId`); a digits-only value after a dotted key that ends like a file extension (`user.pin:1234`); a comparison or other operator between key and value (`password == x`, `password != x`); a session cookie under a name that contains none of the listed words (`connect.sid`; the auth library's `better-auth.session_token` is covered); and the first characters of an email's local part when a clip falls in front of its `@`.
- Nothing forces a handler to call `assertSameOrigin`, `readJsonBody`, or the rate limiter: each is opt-in per handler. When route handlers exist, a test must enumerate every mutation route and prove the origin and content-type checks on each (TM-16).
- The origin check compares against one configured origin. A deployment reachable under two host names (for example with and without `www`) refuses mutations on the second one; it must redirect to the canonical host.
- While a JSON body is read it holds one buffer of at most the limit, and for the length of one copy, when that buffer grows, up to about twice the limit plus the chunk in hand (SEC-020, fixed). A chunk is in memory before the helper sees it, so its size is the runtime's and the proxy's to bound, not the helper's: one oversized chunk is refused after it has arrived.
- The body reader does not yield to the event loop by itself. A source that answers every read without yielding holds the loop until the read ends or is refused: at most the byte limit plus 200,002 reads (SR-05 item 9, SEC-021 fixed), measured at about 80 ms for the full allowance of empty chunks and about 340 ms for a 1 MiB body in one-byte chunks. A body that arrives from the network yields between chunks; whether the framework can hand a route handler a body that does not was not verified.
- An object that passes for bytes without being a typed array (a `Proxy` of a `Uint8Array`, an object with its prototype) is refused with the engine's error and without cancelling the source; a `Proxy` can also make the bytes check itself throw. Both are a 500 with nothing read afterwards and need code inside the server process (observation in SEC-021).
- A chunk made in another JavaScript realm is refused as a server fault even when it is bytes (the check is `instanceof Uint8Array`). It fails closed; if a runtime or framework delivers bodies that way, every request with a body answers 500 until the check is changed.
- The application sets no time limit on reading a body. A client that sends slowly, or stops, holds what it has sent (at most the limit) and one connection for as long as the server and the proxy allow. Request and header timeouts are deployment settings and are not chosen yet.
- The local database image is pinned by digest (SEC-008, fixed) and nothing updates the pin: no dependency bot exists until the CI task (T-070). Until then the image gets security updates only when someone follows "Updating the database image" in `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md`. Local development and tests only.
- A client that drops the connection in the middle of a body is answered as a client error and logged as one `warn` line without a stack (SR-05 item 10; SEC-024 fixed, re-checked on 2026-10-12 against `readJsonBody` and `errorResponse` directly). What remains: every such request still writes one line, so on an endpoint without a rate limit a signed-in account can write `warn` lines as fast as it opens connections; a real fault of the framework in that place is seen only at `warn` (accepted in SR-05 item 10); and whether, and with which error, the framework reports a dropped connection to a route handler was not verified, because no handler reads a body. If the framework fails the request before or outside the read, none of this applies and its own handling does.
- A body that ends early without the stream failing is judged as what arrived. For an object or an array that is always "not JSON"; a bare number cut short is another valid number (`123` becomes `12`). No endpoint accepts a bare scalar as its body, and schemas are strict objects (TM-04), so nothing follows from it today.
- The status and the code of a body that broke off, and the field code of a body nested too deep, are provisional until items 8.2-12 and 8.2-13 of the contract are ruled. Both reuse `invalid_json` today, so a client cannot tell the three cases apart by code, only by message. The security tests pin the status class and the two constants, so a ruling changes no security test.
- A JSON object with the same key twice is accepted and the last value wins (the behaviour of `JSON.parse`; known, not tested in this review). Anything in front of the application that reads the first value sees another request than the application acts on. Not refused; to be looked at with the first proxy or filter that inspects bodies.
- The refusal of the local placeholder passwords (SEC-009) is a guard against a copied file, not a control against intent. It applies only when `NODE_ENV=production`, so a deployed host that runs `next dev` is not refused. It runs when the application starts: the Prisma CLI, the seed, and other scripts read `DATABASE_URL` without it, and the database client reads the variable itself until code-review finding CR-23 moves it to `readDatabaseUrl`. `docker-compose.yml` still falls back to the published passwords for local use, bound to 127.0.0.1. An encoded spelling and `PGPASSWORD` are refused since commit b123ef9 (SEC-025, fixed). Still outside it: the framework does not call the start-up hook when `NEXT_PHASE=phase-production-build` is in the server's environment, and then nothing is validated at all (SEC-029, open); a password file (`PGPASSFILE`, `~/.pgpass`), which the driver reads when neither the URL nor `PGPASSWORD` gives a password, is not looked at (not asked for in SEC-025; the driver calls that path deprecated); and the check is a model of what `pg` 8.23.1 and `pg-connection-string` 2.14.1 do with a connection string, so it must be compared with the driver again when either is updated (`tests/security/round7-recheck.test.ts` carries the model; the comparison itself needs the driver and is run by hand).
- The placeholder check refuses more than the driver would read as a placeholder, in the safe direction: a placeholder anywhere in the text of `DATABASE_URL` (a database or host name included), a placeholder whose escapes the driver would leave alone because another escape in the string is broken, and a placeholder encoded twice in the `password` query parameter. No password that is not built from a published placeholder is refused by it (tested with 18 near misses).
- While the database is down, the public health endpoint writes at most one error-level line per 30 seconds, whatever the number of requests and whatever the reason (SEC-026, fixed), with up to 300 characters of the driver's message, which can name the database host, port, and user. Accepted with the single limit: a failure whose reason differs from the one last written is not written until the interval ends (it is counted, and the next line carries the reason that holds then), and of the request ids of the 503 answers only the first of each line's count is in the log. Observed and not security-relevant: failed checks that fall inside the interval and are followed by a recovery stay in the count, and their first request id stays the one the next failure line carries, however much later that line is written; and a failure whose error object has a `message` that is not a string would be neither counted nor written (by reading; no such error is known from the database layer).
- A label of a broken-off body is written when it has the form of a class name or of an error code (SEC-027, fixed). The form keeps out anything with a blank, an `@`, a colon, a slash, a quote, or a non-ASCII letter, and anything over 64 characters. It does not keep out a single token: a digits-only code can be a phone number written without separators, and a dotted one an IPv4 address or a host name. Accepted, because both properties are set by the runtime today; **condition:** when the multipart reader of T-041 passes a form parser's errors to `requestBodyAborted`, that parser's `name` and `code` values are reviewed first.
- Requests refused before the rate limit (steps 1 to 4 of SR-05 item 5) use no allowance, and most endpoints have no rate limit at all. The application therefore does not bound how many requests a client sends, only what each may cost: no body is read for a caller that will be refused, and a body is at most its limit. A flood of cheap requests is for the proxy or the host to stop, and nothing is planned there.
- A signed-in account can send bodies up to the limit to every mutation that has no rate limit (cart, addresses, product edits) as fast as it likes. The cost per request is bounded (SR-05); the number of requests is not.
- A cart line whose product is hidden still shows that product's current title, slug, and picture: registered as SEC-054 (open) since the cart was built (T-031, reviewed 2026-10-14).
- Cart (T-031, reviewed 2026-10-14, [reviews/T-031-security.md](reviews/T-031-security.md)): ownership of a line id, mass assignment, quantity bounds, and server-side amounts were tested at unit level against an in-memory stand-in for the database client. The cart lock, the transaction, the `CartItem` constraints, and every concurrency case rest on `tests/integration/cart`, which did not exist at review time (code-review CR-T031-01) and was not run by security. A cart holds at most 50 lines through `POST /cart/items` (SEC-053, fixed and re-checked 2026-10-22). Items a failed payment returns (`returnItemToCart`) are merged without the cap, so lines added while a payment was open can take a cart above 50 (shown in `tests/security/t032-checkout.itest.ts`, 55 lines); the cart then refuses new lines and checkout until lines are removed, and the overflow is bounded by the lines of the orders open at that moment. Carts stored above 50 before 3e2f4d1 keep their lines; `GET /cart` still reads all of them. Cart endpoints have no rate limit by contract; a signed-in account can add, change, and read as fast as it likes, each write taking one transaction and one connection from the pool. The cart was reviewed at HEAD while backend-api was changing `src/modules/cart` (CR-T031-02 to -06); those later commits were run against the same tests but not read in full.
- A restore of a database backup undoes everything decided since the backup: ended sessions are valid again and deactivations, suspensions, and takedowns are lifted. SR-07 "Restore" requires every session deleted and every restriction back in force before the application serves; when the replaced database cannot be read, the restrictions can be reconstructed only as far as the server logs reach, and the audit log entries since the backup are lost in any case.
- An unpaid order holds its stock until the payment timeout, and an account may keep creating such orders. Short-lived with the mock provider by design; with a real provider a bound on unpaid orders per account is needed ("Scheduled work: security minimums"). The sweep is started by the start-up hook (SEC-055, fixed 2026-10-14) and fails at most 100 overdue orders a minute; it runs only while the server process runs, so a host that stops idle containers stops it without an error (deployment configuration).
- Checkout, orders, and mock payment (T-032, reviewed 2026-10-14, [reviews/T-032-security.md](reviews/T-032-security.md)): ownership, server-computed amounts, the guarded decrement, idempotency, and the payment guards were tested on real PostgreSQL with the handlers called in process, not through a running server. Not covered: the full race suite and the transaction limits under load (T-033); lock hold time with long carts (bounded at 50 lines since SEC-053 was fixed; not measured); seller and admin order views, failed orders hidden from sellers (BR-20), and the UI labelling of simulated payments, none of which exists yet. With the mock provider the client chooses whether an order is paid (D-011). Since 3e2f4d1 the mock is refused when `NODE_ENV=production` (SEC-056, fixed and re-checked 2026-10-22); that control depends on deployment configuration: a server run with `next dev`, or with `NODE_ENV` set to anything else, uses the mock. `GET /orders` has no rate limit, by contract.
- Seller application and store (T-040, reviewed 2026-10-15, [reviews/T-040-security.md](reviews/T-040-security.md)): role and ownership, the store lock, the order of refusals, and the name rule were tested with the handlers and services called in process on real PostgreSQL, not through a running server. Not covered: the admin endpoints that expose approve and reject (T-050, not built; the services check `ADMIN` themselves); upload content behind a `logoKey` (T-041, not built; a logo is any upload row of the store); the store logo and description as rendered on the storefront beyond the `UserName` isolation of the name. Standing: store names are unique by key and renames are recorded since D-041 (SEC-058 fixed, re-checked 2026-10-15; what remains is the next item); a seller who holds the `FOR SHARE` lock of `requireApprovedStoreForWrite` and then updates the `Store` row in the same transaction can deadlock with another such writer (lock upgrade), so later tasks must not update the store row after that call, or must lock it `FOR NO KEY UPDATE` instead; `requireApprovedStoreForWrite` does not check the owner's `isActive`, so a request that read its actor just before a deactivation committed can still finish one write (the window is one request; deactivation deletes sessions).
- Store names after D-041 (SEC-058 re-check 2026-10-15, [reviews/SEC-058-recheck.md](reviews/SEC-058-recheck.md)):
  - Look-alike letters of other scripts are not caught (Cyrillic U+0430 for the Latin `a` of `admin`, Greek omicron, dotless i, Turkish dotted I): accepted by the owner in D-041 point 1. Same-script Thai copies and U+2800 were registered as SEC-060 and are refused at validation since D-042 point 2 (re-checked 2026-10-19). Still open: sara e, U+FE0E or U+FE0F, sara e (SEC-061), narrow spaces inside a word (SEC-062), Thai look-alike letters U+0E45, U+0E03, U+0E05 (SEC-063).
  - Punctuation and symbols are kept in the key, so `Shop`, `Shop.`, `Shop!`, and `Shop/` with U+2044 are four names; `0` and `O`, `rn` and `m` differ too. Two names that differ only that way can both exist. Not ruled on. The reserved-name side is fixed for punctuation and symbols since D-042 point 1 (SEC-059, re-checked 2026-10-19), but `Adrnin`, `0fficial`, `Adm1n`, `Offícial`, and `ADMİN` (Turkish dotted I, which folds to `i` plus U+0307) still pass the reserved match.
  - Reserved names since D-042 point 1 are refused when contained anywhere, so ordinary names are refused too: `Badminton Pro`, `Unofficial Fan Shop`, and Thai names containing `ทางการ` inside a compound (agricultural, medical, trade). Accepted by the owner; the list in `RESERVED_STORE_NAMES` is the only control of how many ordinary names are refused.
  - Since D-042 point 2 every field that uses `NAME_ALLOWED_TEXT` refuses any combining mark repeated within a run, in every script, which is wider than the ruling's "repeated tone mark" (re-check 2026-10-19). No counter-example found in Thai; other scripts were not surveyed. Canonically equivalent repeats (`e` + U+0301 + U+0341, precomposed `é` + U+0301) are not caught; Latin fonts usually draw them visibly. U+16FE4 (a `Mn` filler, not default-ignorable) is accepted inside a word; whether it draws as nothing depends on the font (not checked in a browser).
  - The key depends on PostgreSQL's Unicode tables (`normalize`, `casefold`, the `pg_unicode_fast` collation), which change with major versions. `store_name_key` is declared `IMMUTABLE` and the column is `STORED`, so after a major upgrade (pg_upgrade or a dump and restore that keeps the stored values) old rows keep keys computed with the old tables while new writes use the new ones: two names that the new tables fold together can then both exist, and a rebuild of the index can fail. After every major upgrade: recompute the keys (`UPDATE "Store" SET "name" = "name"`), look for duplicates, and `REINDEX INDEX "Store_nameKey_key"`. This is a deployment procedure; nothing enforces it.
  - The migration renames colliding existing stores (`<name> (<slug>)`) without an `AuditLog` row (no actor exists in a migration) and only reports a count; such a renamed name can be longer than `STORE_NAME_MAX` (100; no database check). A migration that fails on a remaining collision prints the colliding key, a store name, in the deploy output. Existing stores whose name is reserved are not touched by the migration.
  - The application check (`checkStoreName` in `insertApplication`) is a read without a lock, and pending applications are not compared with each other. Two applicants can hold applications for one name; the second approval is refused `409 STORE_NAME_TAKEN` by the index and the admin must reject it. The index is the control; the application check only gives an early answer.
  - `409 STORE_NAME_TAKEN` tells the caller that some store, including a suspended or hidden one, has that key (judged acceptable in SEC-058's re-check evidence: bounded by the `seller-application` limit for customers; for sellers each miss is a real rename with an audit row; no rename rate limit, D-041 point 4).
  - A rename takes the store row `FOR UPDATE` (the unique key changes), which conflicts with `FOR SHARE` (`requireApprovedStoreForWrite`) and with the `FOR KEY SHARE` that foreign-key checks take when rows referencing the store are inserted (products, order rows). While a rename runs those wait, and a rename waits for them; tested only against `requireApprovedStoreForWrite` (waits, then succeeds, no deadlock). Two stores swapping names at the same instant can deadlock in the unique index; PostgreSQL then aborts one, which answers 500 (not observed in 3 runs; fails closed).
  - Rename history is in `AuditLog` only; no admin view exists until T-050.
- The blind resend of a refused checkout is prevented by the client only until item 8.2-1 of the contract is ruled on (position above; no ruling yet).
- Log retention and access (SR-06) depend entirely on deployment configuration: the retention setting of whatever stores the logs and of their backups, which named people hold accounts there, and whether the proxy's own access logs follow the same rule. None of it exists yet.
- 90 days is a maximum and the owner set no minimum (D-026, OQ-48). A host that keeps logs only briefly, or loses them at each release, is within the rule, and an incident found later than its logs lasted cannot be investigated from them. How long logs are actually kept is a deployment choice that has not been made.
- The investigation exception (SR-06 item 8) is a manual procedure: someone has to notice before day 90 that lines are evidence, copy only those, and write the record. Nothing enforces "only the relevant lines" or the deletion when the investigation closes except that record. Who may open and close such a hold is not named.
- Session removal (SR-07) is not designed. The bound is one day after expiry (D-026, OQ-49), and the MVP has no scheduled jobs: until T-015 chooses a mechanism that runs daily without traffic, the bound has nothing behind it. A clean-up job that stops silently breaks it unnoticed.
- Database backups are kept 30 days (D-026, OQ-50) and keep the address and browser details of sessions that existed when the backup was taken, for up to 30 days after the session itself is gone. Restoring a backup brings removed session rows back, which is why a restore ends with deleting every session (SR-07 "Restore"). No backup exists yet, so neither the 30 days nor who can read a backup is implemented.
- Suspending a store ends no session (D-027). What stops a suspended seller is only the status check on each request (TM-26); a seller service that forgets the check, or reads the status from a cached copy, leaves a suspended store acting with a valid session. To be proven per service when the seller module exists. Proven for `PATCH /seller/store` on 2026-10-15 (T-040 review): the actor is read from the database per request, and the write takes the store row lock and carries `status = 'APPROVED'`, so a suspension committed before or during the request refuses it. Every later seller write (T-041, T-042) must call `requireApprovedStoreForWrite` first inside its transaction; nothing enforces that yet. The seller order status change of T-043 is the intended exception: a suspended store still fulfils and cancels its paid orders (BR-14), so it carries no store-status check (reviewed 2026-10-20; `t043-seller-orders.test.ts` fails if one is added).
- The sign-in address stays in the server log for up to 90 days after the session it belonged to is gone. This follows from the two owner decisions together and is intended (SR-07 item 6).
- The wall clock drives rate-limit windows; a backward clock step lengthens windows and delays sweeps (fails towards refusing).
- No multi-factor authentication, including for admins.
- No email verification or password recovery until an email provider exists.
- No web application firewall, bot protection, or fraud detection.
- CSP strictness is limited by what the framework's inline scripts require.
- Local-disk file storage has no malware scanning.
- **Uploads (T-041, reviewed 2026-10-19, [reviews/T-041-security.md](reviews/T-041-security.md)).** Not reviewed or not observed: requests through a running `next start` (the framework's decoding of the `[key]` segment, its own body limit for route handlers, its chunk sizes, and how long it lets a body stall were not observed; handlers were called in process); browser behaviour with the served headers (nosniff and the sandbox CSP were read from the response, not tried in a browser); Windows-specific file names on a real deployment volume. Accepted by design or pending a ruling: polyglot files with valid leading bytes are stored and served as images (control: recorded type, `nosniff`, `default-src 'none'; sandbox`; a browser that ignores `nosniff` is not covered); files are served from the application's own origin, so a future bypass of those headers would run on that origin (a separate origin for user files is a deployment option); a suspended store's pictures stay public (superseded by SEC-068 item 1 once branch `backend/sec-068-serving` is merged: then only its order item pictures stay public, SEC-071); a body is read in full (up to 5,259,264 bytes) before parsing, so 30 concurrent requests of one seller hold about 158 MB, and a stalled body is bounded only by the Node server's request timeout (deployment, DEPLOYMENT.md); `GET /api/files/{key}` has no rate limit and, with `no-store`, every view reaches the application and the database; if `UPLOAD_DIR` is missing outside production, every file request, valid key or not, is a 500 with an error-level line. Open: SEC-064, SEC-065 (PARTIAL since the re-check of 2026-10-20), SEC-066. SEC-067 FIXED (re-checked 2026-10-20); its index plans were checked on near-empty test tables only.
- **Upload lifetime (SEC-065 re-check, 2026-10-20, [reviews/SEC-065-recheck.md](reviews/SEC-065-recheck.md)).** The cleanup schedule is wired into `src/instrumentation.ts` (37d07c8) and its scheduled deletion was reviewed on 2026-10-20 ([reviews/SEC-065-cleanup-security.md](reviews/SEC-065-cleanup-security.md)); it was not observed in a running `next start` or on a deployment volume. Depends on deployment: every process that runs the job must see the same `UPLOAD_DIR` volume, mounted before the start. Since 8a57d53 (SEC-073, re-checked 2026-10-21), a root without the marker `.marketplace-uploads` deletes no row and logs one error line per run, and a file already missing is logged as a `filesMissing` count. **Owner decision, recorded and not fixed:** `put` creates the root and the marker when they are missing. So if a seller uploads while the volume is not mounted, the upload and the marker land on the empty mount point, and the cleanup accepts that root from then on. Once the volume is mounted over it, the cleanup runs against the real volume again. While the volume is unmounted, the rows of the real files are deleted and each missing file only raises the `filesMissing` warn line. Options for the owner: (a) accept, relying on devops alerts on `filesMissing`; (b) never create the marker from `put`, and write it once at provisioning (for example `pnpm uploads:init`), so an unmounted mount point never gets one; (c) store an id in the marker that the database also holds, and compare the two. Pinned as "(documented)" in `tests/security/sec073-recheck.test.ts`. A root used before 8a57d53 gets the marker only at its next upload; until then the cleanup refuses to run there and logs an error line every hour. Devops alerting on `filesMissing` and on the failure line (SEC-073 item 3) is not built. Accepted as outside the app's attacker model: the path check is lexical, so a two-digit directory replaced by a link or junction by someone with write access to `UPLOAD_DIR` is followed. With several application processes, each runs its own schedule; `SKIP LOCKED` was shown to keep two concurrent runs from deleting a row twice, not tested across processes. The product picture attach and detach paths of `src/modules/inventory`, not reviewed at the time, were reviewed on 2026-10-20 (T-042): no limit on products exists and pictures of draft, rejected, and taken-down products are served, so attaching to throw-away drafts does bypass the cap for free hosting (SEC-068). The grace period counts from the upload's creation, not from when it was last detached, so a picture removed from a product or replaced as a logo after 24 hours is 404 at once and deleted at the next run (contract wording says "nothing references for longer than a grace period"; judged not a security issue). The race in which an attach commits between the cleanup's first snapshot and its row lock is covered by the second statement's re-check by reading only; it was not forced in a test. No admin takedown of an upload exists (SEC-065 item 4).
- **Raw error objects in the logger (CR-T040-07, 2026-10-19).** `errorResponse` logs class name, status, and code only, so Prisma `meta` (and its `DETAIL` with the conflicting value) never reaches the log through it. The logger itself writes an error's message, stack, and every own property, `meta` included, when a call site passes the error object; no call site does today, and a static test in `tests/security/t041-uploads.test.ts` fails if one starts to. That test matches the usual shapes, not every possible one.
- **Seller products and inventory (T-042, reviewed 2026-10-20, [reviews/T-042-security.md](reviews/T-042-security.md)).** Not reviewed: any page or component that renders seller product text (none exists; the schema accepts markup as text, so escaping on output is the control, and the seller dashboard of T-043 must not use raw HTML); the admin moderation side (T-050/T-051: whether an approval checks `contentRevision`, whether the queue shows the slug, SEC-070); requests through a running `next start`. Accepted by contract, not ruled by security: no rate limit on product writes (section 6 lists none; SEC-069), no `AuditLog` row for seller product writes, a store logo may also be a product picture. The second layer of the ownership control (the store condition in every guarded write) was shown by one mutant: with the product lock unscoped, a foreign `PATCH` is refused 403 and writes nothing (the 404 then becomes a 403, which tells the id exists). Price and stock races were exercised with 20 and 8 parallel requests in one process, not across processes or under load.
- **File serving by owner (SEC-068 item 1 re-check, 2026-10-20, branch `sec-068-serving-tests`, not merged).** Until the branch is merged, main still serves every registered upload that is not an expired orphan to anyone. After the merge: the response to a non-public file depends on the session, so `Cache-Control: no-store` (provisional in API_CONTRACTS 5.8.1) must not become a shared-cache value without `private` or a separate rule for non-public files; the admin moderation screens cannot show pictures of products awaiting approval (T-050 must rule an admin read); an order item's picture stays public after a takedown (SEC-071); a public file is decided without a session lookup and a non-public one with one, so response time differs by kind (judged not useful to an attacker: keys are random and a refusal is the unknown-key 404); not observed through `next start`.
- **Seller orders and summary (T-043, reviewed 2026-10-20, [reviews/T-043-security.md](reviews/T-043-security.md)).** Static checks only were written by security; the database behaviour (cross-store and unpaid 404, suspended store, the two races) rests on the author's integration tests, run once by this review, not written or mutated by it. Accepted by the owner (2026-10-20): no `AuditLog` row and no reason for a seller's status change or cancel of a paid order, so who cancelled and why cannot be shown from the database; the payment stays `PAID` and the refund happens outside the system (BR-21). Accepted by contract: no rate limit on these endpoints (section 6). Pending: the list drops `paidAt` and the buyer's address (owner ruling on Q42; backend-api's change was uncommitted at review and is to be re-checked). Not reviewed: any page rendering seller order data (T-044); `carrierName` and `trackingNumber` accept markup and `javascript:` text, so pages must render them as escaped text, never as a link target or raw HTML; the admin fulfilment side (T-050); requests through `next start`. Open: SEC-072.
- **Seller isolation suite (T-045, reviewed 2026-10-21, commit de61504).** What the review established:
  - The suite and `tests/security/t045-isolation-gaps.itest.ts` call route modules in process with real sessions and real PostgreSQL. Nothing was observed under `next start`.
  - Each of the store-scope controls that the mutations removed is caught by at least one integration file.
  - Scope clauses that pair `storeId` with `ownerId` are redundant by design. Removing only one of the pair is not detectable by any test, and was not tried.

  Not covered:
  - Timing differences between a foreign id and a missing id.
  - A seller whose store has been suspended, against foreign ids. The scope is the same, but this was not run.
  - `POST /seller/uploads` under mutation.
  - `GET /api/files/{key}` as a cross-seller read. It is covered by `tests/integration/uploads/endpoints.test.ts` line 929, which was not re-run under mutation.
  - The seller dashboard pages under `src/app/seller/`. Hidden buttons are not a control, and each page's data comes from the same services.
- **e2e harness isolation (SEC-074 to SEC-082, re-checked 2026-10-22).** What protects the e2e server's database connection from a planted env file is the empty `PGOPTIONS` and `NODE_PG_FORCE_NATIVE` in `webServer.env`. That was shown on Windows through Playwright's shell, `pnpm exec`, and `next dev`. It was not run under a POSIX shell or in CI. The file scanner is a second layer. SEC-078 and SEC-079 are fixed: since 76e43d8 the scanner parses each file with the server's own `@next/env`, so parsing cannot disagree. SEC-080 is fixed (c2bf1f9): driver values are expanded against the real environment as the server expands them. A `DATABASE_URL` written as `$X` or `${X}` is still read as empty and not compared (SEC-082, open). The scanner expands against the Playwright process's environment, not the server's merged `webServer.env`; for the driver names this does not matter because `webServer.env` empties them. No e2e suite was run after 76e43d8. Other `PG*` variables in a `.env*` file (for example `PGSSLMODE` or `PGPASSFILE`) are not emptied for the e2e server. They are not in `REFUSED_ENVIRONMENT_VARIABLES`. `tests/helpers/test-database-url.ts:345-346` explains why the host, port, user, and database variables are inert under a strict URL. That reasoning was not re-checked here for the other `PG*` names `pg` reads. The canary proves the server's database once, before the sign-up. A same-user process that takes `127.0.0.1:3100` after that check is not detected. `pnpm dev` still binds every interface. Concurrent e2e runs share `marketplace_e2e` and port 3100; the second run is refused by Playwright's port check, as observed twice in this session.
- **Admin module (T-050, reviewed 2026-10-23).** Accepted by design or left open:
  - Any active admin may deactivate any other admin; only self-deactivation and the last active admin are refused (REQ-A-02). One rogue admin can remove every other admin, leaving itself the last; the audit log records it, nothing prevents it. Admins are created only by the bootstrap script (D-025).
  - The audit log is append-only by code convention (DATA_MODEL.md 6.17). The application's database role may `UPDATE` and `DELETE` `AuditLog`; no trigger or grant stops it. Anyone with the database credentials can rewrite the record. A trigger refusing `UPDATE` and `DELETE`, or a separate role without those grants, would close this; it is a deployment and database decision.
  - An admin may cancel a seller order whose payment is still `PENDING`; its stock comes back at once, and if the payment then succeeds the order is `PAID` with a cancelled part and no refund in the system (BR-21, D-028). The author's suite covers this.
  - No rate limit applies to admin endpoints, and the report range (`from`, `to`) is unbounded; each report and the summary aggregate over whole tables. Admin-only, so judged acceptable.
  - Admin views show names, emails, phones, delivery addresses, and review texts; none is logged by the module. The admin pages that render them (T-051) were reviewed on 2026-10-23 ([reviews/T-051-security.md](reviews/T-051-security.md)): text renders escaped and isolated. The list pages hand whole DTOs to Client Components, so the page payload carries fields the list does not show (for example the delivery address of every order on an order list page, up to 50); the viewer is an admin who may open each of them anyway, so this is data minimisation, not a finding. Not observed: the `Cache-Control` of admin HTML under `next start` with Cache Components and partial prefetching on, the browser's back-forward cache and router cache after sign-out, and the pages in a real browser. `renderForAdmin` turns any failed read into the error state without writing a log line, so a failing admin page leaves no server trace (operational, not a control).
  - SEC-090 (High) is fixed by BR-41 (re-checked 2026-10-23). Accepted with the rule: any active admin reads every picture attached to any product, drafts never published included, with no audit entry for the read (BR-41 (4)); a seller who also uses its logo as a product picture makes that logo readable by admins while its store is not public. Not observed: the review page's pictures in a real browser or under `next start`.
  - SEC-088 (deactivated admin's in-flight request) is open pending the owner's reading of D-024.
- No independent human penetration test is planned within this project.
- Real payment integration will introduce webhook verification, replay protection, and reconciliation risks that the mock does not exercise.
- No dedicated secret scanner is installed locally or in CI yet.

## Reporting a vulnerability

See [.github/SECURITY.md](../.github/SECURITY.md).
